ToxicChat validation confirms the adapted vs.\\ vanilla gap transfers to human-written attacks; multi-turn human and multi-LLM data remain future work.",
    "originalFile": "% conclusion.tex — compressed for 8-page limit\n\n\\section{Discussion}\n\\label{sec:discussion}\n\n\\paragraph{Topic shortcut confound.}\nThree observations argue against a topic-level shortcut explaining detection: (1) topic classification yields unstable OOD generalization (F1\\,=\\,0.541$\\pm$0.207 on DD) and underperforms unsupervised alternatives; (2) scrambled labels achieve detection equivalent to correct labels (F1 gap $\\leq$3.9\\,pp); (3) MLM without labels achieves strong detection (F1\\,$\\geq$\\,0.97 at $K \\leq 5$ on DD). The benefit comes from domain exposure, not classification-based shortcuts.\n\n\\paragraph{Single-LLM confound.}\nBoth training and synthetic OOD data are generated by Qwen3-8B. ToxicChat~\\citep{lin2023toxicchat} (204 human-written jailbreaks) provides partial resolution: all domain-adapted variants outperform vanilla by 18--42\\,pp (Table~\\ref{tab:human_data}). A cross-LLM experiment (Appendix~\\ref{app:cross_llm}) further isolates the confound: with Llama-3.1-8B jailbreaks and Qwen benign text, detection F1 recovers to 0.80--1.00; the remaining ActorAttack gap (0.80 vs.\\ 1.00) reflects genuine cross-LLM variation rather than Qwen-specific artifacts. Multi-turn human data is needed to confirm the complete hierarchy.\n\n\\paragraph{Variance reversal on human data.}\nOn synthetic OOD, classification supervision reduces cross-seed variance 4--20$\\times$ over MLM; on ToxicChat, this reverses (9-class std\\,=\\,0.087 vs.\\ JB-MLM 0.013). Classification stabilizes within-distribution predictions, while MLM generalizes across distributions.\n\n\\paragraph{Encoder capacity confound.}\nDeBERTa-v3-base (184M), RoBERTa-base (125M), and RoBERTa-large (355M) span a 2.8$\\times$ capacity range but also differ in pretraining objectives. The consistent capacity gradient across three models (Section~\\ref{sec:cross_encoder}) suggests that model size is the dominant factor, though we cannot fully rule out architectural contributions.\n\n\\section{Conclusion}\n\\label{sec:conclusion}\n\nOur ablation across two encoders and three OOD attack families yields three findings. First, for base-size encoders (125--184M), DAPT bridges $\\sim$60\\,pp of the OOD gap without labels; for the largest encoder tested (355M), pretrained representations already suffice on structurally complex attacks. Second, three encoder scales reveal a capacity gradient: as model size increases, the set of attacks requiring domain adaptation narrows. Third, classification supervision stabilizes within-distribution predictions (variance reduced 4--20$\\times$) regardless of label correctness, but does not transfer to human data (Section~\\ref{sec:human_data}). Validation on ToxicChat confirms the adapted vs.\\ vanilla gap transfers to human-written attacks; multi-turn human validation and multi-LLM training data remain future work.\n\n\\section{Limitations}\n\\label{sec:limitations}\n\n\\paragraph{Dataset scale and diversity.}\nThe training set contains 500 synthetic conversations generated by a single model (Qwen3-8B), and all multi-turn OOD evaluation data is also Qwen3-8B-generated. This scale limits statistical power: the perturbation evaluation ($n$=76) means tier boundaries in Table~\\ref{tab:perturbation} correspond to 1--3 misclassifications. While ToxicChat (human-authored, single-turn) confirms that DAPT's detection advantage transfers across data sources (+18--42\\,pp over vanilla; Section~\\ref{sec:human_data}), multi-turn generalization across LLM generation sources remains to be verified. Whether the full evidence hierarchy, including classification supervision's within-distribution stabilization, generalizes to human-generated multi-turn attacks is open.\nCross-LLM validation with Llama-3.1-8B-Instruct as the target model reveals that jailbreak detection transfers across LLMs (F1 recovers to 0.80--1.00 when the benign distribution is held constant), but the benign baseline is LLM-specific: replacing Qwen benign text with Llama benign text causes false-positive rates to spike (F1 drops to 0.37--0.43). A per-class error decomposition (Appendix~\\ref{app:cross_llm}, Table~\\ref{tab:cross_llm_error}) confirms that true positive rates are identical across benign conditions; the F1 collapse is driven entirely by false positives on unfamiliar benign text. The residual cross-LLM degradation on structurally complex attacks (ActorAttack F1 = 0.80 vs.\\ 0.997 in-distribution) indicates that some jailbreak features remain source-specific. Multi-LLM training data is a clear direction for future work (Appendix~\\ref{app:cross_llm}).\n\n\\paragraph{Lexical separability.}\nTF-IDF logistic regression achieves F1 = 0.961 on OOD Deceptive Delight with near-perfect per-prefix performance; this indicates substantial lexical separability on this attack family. On DD alone, we cannot distinguish whether continued pretraining captures beyond-lexical features or exploits the same lexical cues. ActorAttack resolves this question: TF-IDF collapses to F1 = 0.261 while domain-adapted models maintain F1 $\\geq$ 0.89, a $\\geq$63\\,pp gap that demonstrates continued pretraining captures features beyond lexical overlap on structurally complex attacks. Whether this beyond-lexical advantage extends to other structurally complex attack families beyond ActorAttack requires further evaluation.\n\n\\paragraph{Strategy annotation quality.}\nPersuasion strategy labels are produced by Qwen3-8B with structured prompting. Inter-annotator agreement with a human sample ($n$=50) yields Cohen's $\\kappa$=0.78, which indicates substantial but imperfect agreement. The small human validation sample and reliance on a single annotator model limit confidence in label quality. We plan to release the annotation prompts and data to enable independent verification.\n\n\\paragraph{OOD strategy prediction.}\nWhile the 9-class model achieves high detection F1 on OOD attacks (0.997$\\pm$0.005 on Deceptive Delight), its strategy-level predictions do not generalize: OOD strategy accuracy is 30.7\\% ($\\kappa$=0.126). The interpretability provided by the persuasion taxonomy is currently limited to known attack families where strategy labels are reliable.\n\n\\paragraph{FITD discriminative power.}\nFITD provides minimal discriminative signal: both vanilla and 9-class achieve F1 $\\geq$ 0.990 at all prefixes. FITD serves primarily as a confirmation that simple OOD attacks do not challenge any variant, rather than as a meaningful evaluation axis.\n\n\\paragraph{Scope.}\nOnly persuasion-mediated multi-turn attacks fall within scope. Single-turn token-level attacks~\\citep{zou2023gcg}, non-persuasive multi-turn strategies, and attacks targeting modalities other than text are not addressed. The user-only input assumption (excluding assistant responses) may miss information in attacks where the model's own responses contribute to the escalation trajectory.\n\n\\paragraph{Computation cost.}\nStage~1 continued pretraining requires approximately 30 minutes on a single A100 GPU per variant. Stage~2 training completes in under 5 minutes. Total pipeline cost for all seven variants is under 4 GPU-hours, making the approach accessible for replication.\n",

_source line 2483 · 2026-05-19T09:04:34.767Z_

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_01DaWwiV3eqoww8pxzj9movu`</summary>

```text
58	On OOD DD, vanilla DeBERTa collapses to F1 = 0.312$\pm$0.026, worsening with more turns (0.628$\pm$0.038 at $K$=1 to 0.312$\pm$0.026 at Full), consistent with the finding that attack turns occupy ``benign'' embedding regions~\citep{bullwinkel2025repeng}.
59	MLM achieves mean F1 $\geq$ 0.97 at all prefixes $K \leq 5$ without any classification labels; domain exposure alone is sufficient.
60	At full conversation length, MLM drops to 0.914$\pm$0.115 (seed 456 recall = 0.64), while the 9-class model maintains 0.997$\pm$0.005.
61	TF-IDF LR reaches F1 = 0.961 on full DD with near-perfect per-prefix performance ($K$=1--5: 0.994--1.000); DD is thus fully solvable by lexical features alone.
62	On structurally complex ActorAttack, TF-IDF collapses to F1 = 0.261 (precision = 1.0, recall = 0.15), while 9-class maintains 0.972$\pm$0.026: a 71\,pp gap. Lexical methods cannot capture multi-persona role-playing attack structure. This establishes a ``difficulty threshold'' for lexical methods parallel to that of vanilla encoders: both succeed on DD and fail on ActorAttack (Section~\ref{sec:limitations}).
63	Bootstrap CIs confirm these gaps: 9-class DD Full 95\% CI [0.943, 1.000] vs.\ vanilla [0.202, 0.341]; 9-class ActorAttack Full CI [0.856, 1.000] vs.\ vanilla [0.203, 0.314]. Paired permutation tests yield $p < 0.0001$ for all prefix lengths on both datasets.
64	FITD is easier: 9-class and vanilla both reach F1 $\geq$ 0.990 at all prefixes.
65	
66	\begin{figure}[t]
67	\centering
68	\includegraphics[width=\columnwidth]{figures/fig2_cross_attack_bar.pdf}
69	\caption{OOD Deceptive Delight F1 across prefix lengths for three representative variants. Vanilla DeBERTa degrades monotonically as more (benign-appearing) attack turns accumulate. MLM maintains high F1 at early prefixes but becomes unstable at full length. The 9-class model remains stable across all prefixes.}
70	\label{fig:cross_attack_bar}
71	\end{figure}
72

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_01NL7P4veW5f4wvdJxFjqpgx`</summary>

```text
175	RoBERTa-large (355M) reveals a capacity-DAPT interaction: while domain adaptation improves DD detection across all scales (+6\,pp for large, +69\,pp for DeBERTa), its effect on ActorAttack reverses for the largest encoder (vanilla 1.000 vs.\ 9-class 0.946). Smaller encoders (125--184M) require DAPT to bridge a 47--71\,pp gap on ActorAttack. DAPT hyperparameters were not tuned for RoBERTa-large (identical learning rate and epoch count to base models), so the observed regression may partly reflect suboptimal adaptation.
176	
177	Three encoder scales thus reveal a capacity gradient for the \emph{difficulty threshold}: DeBERTa-v3-base requires adaptation for all unseen attacks, RoBERTa-base tolerates simple attacks but fails on complex ones, and RoBERTa-large handles both without adaptation. Although pretraining objectives also differ (RTD vs.\ MLM), the consistent monotonic pattern suggests model size is the dominant factor (Section~\ref{sec:discussion}).
178	Classification supervision provides a consistent stability benefit across both base encoders (cross-seed variance: 0.005--0.046 vs.\ 0.026--0.201).
179	
180	Centered CKA~\citep{kornblith2019cka} confirms that both DAPT and classification supervision substantially reshape representations, yet comparable CKA shifts yield different detection gains depending on the starting point (Appendix~\ref{sec:cka_heatmap}).
181	Linear probing on diagnostic tasks shows 9-class outperforms vanilla and MLM by 1--5\,pp; MLM reshapes representations without improving diagnostic separability.
182	
183	%----------------------------------------------------------------------
184	\subsection{Human Data Validation}
185	\label{sec:human_data}
186	
187	Since both training and OOD evaluation data are Qwen3-8B-generated, we validate on ToxicChat~\citep{lin2023toxicchat} (204 human-written jailbreak attempts + 204 benign conversations from real LMSYS interactions).
188	
189	\begin{table}[t]
```

    "oldString": "Three observations argue against a topic-level shortcut explaining detection: (1) topic classification yields unstable OOD generalization (F1\\,=\\,0.541$\\pm$0.207 on DD) and underperforms unsupervised alternatives; (2) scrambled labels achieve detection equivalent to correct labels (F1 gap $\\leq$3.9\\,pp); (3) MLM without labels achieves strong detection (F1\\,$\\geq$\\,0.97 at $K \\leq 5$ on DD). The benefit comes from domain exposure, not classification-based shortcuts.",
    "newString": "Three observations argue against a topic-level shortcut: (1) topic classification yields unstable OOD generalization (F1\\,=\\,0.541$\\pm$0.207 on DD); (2) scrambled labels match correct labels (F1 gap $\\leq$3.9\\,pp); (3) MLM achieves strong detection without labels (F1\\,$\\geq$\\,0.97 at $K \\leq 5$). Domain exposure, not classification, drives detection.",
    "originalFile": "% conclusion.tex — compressed for 8-page limit\n\n\\section{Discussion}\n\\label{sec:discussion}\n\n\\paragraph{Topic shortcut confound.}\nThree observations argue against a topic-level shortcut explaining detection: (1) topic classification yields unstable OOD generalization (F1\\,=\\,0.541$\\pm$0.207 on DD) and underperforms unsupervised alternatives; (2) scrambled labels achieve detection equivalent to correct labels (F1 gap $\\leq$3.9\\,pp); (3) MLM without labels achieves strong detection (F1\\,$\\geq$\\,0.97 at $K \\leq 5$ on DD). The benefit comes from domain exposure, not classification-based shortcuts.\n\n\\paragraph{Single-LLM confound.}\nBoth training and synthetic OOD data are generated by Qwen3-8B. ToxicChat~\\citep{lin2023toxicchat} (204 human-written jailbreaks) provides partial resolution: all domain-adapted variants outperform vanilla by 18--42\\,pp (Table~\\ref{tab:human_data}). A cross-LLM experiment (Appendix~\\ref{app:cross_llm}) further isolates the confound: with Llama-3.1-8B jailbreaks and Qwen benign text, detection F1 recovers to 0.80--1.00; the remaining ActorAttack gap (0.80 vs.\\ 1.00) reflects genuine cross-LLM variation. Multi-turn human data is needed to confirm the complete hierarchy.\n\n\\paragraph{Variance reversal on human data.}\nOn synthetic OOD, classification supervision reduces cross-seed variance 4--20$\\times$ over MLM; on ToxicChat, this reverses (9-class std\\,=\\,0.087 vs.\\ JB-MLM 0.013). Classification stabilizes within-distribution predictions, while MLM generalizes across distributions.\n\n\\paragraph{Encoder capacity confound.}\nDeBERTa-v3-base (184M), RoBERTa-base (125M), and RoBERTa-large (355M) span a 2.8$\\times$ capacity range but also differ in pretraining objectives. The consistent capacity gradient across three models (Section~\\ref{sec:cross_encoder}) suggests that model size is the dominant factor, though we cannot fully rule out architectural contributions.\n\n\\section{Conclusion}\n\\label{sec:conclusion}\n\nOur ablation across three encoder scales and three OOD attack families yields three findings. First, for base-size encoders (125--184M), DAPT bridges $\\sim$60\\,pp of the OOD gap without labels; for the largest encoder (355M), pretrained representations already suffice on structurally complex attacks. Second, as model size increases, the set of attacks requiring domain adaptation narrows. Third, classification supervision stabilizes within-distribution predictions (variance reduced 4--20$\\times$) but does not transfer to human data. ToxicChat validation confirms the adapted vs.\\ vanilla gap transfers to human-written attacks; multi-turn human and multi-LLM data remain future work.\n\n\\section{Limitations}\n\\label{sec:limitations}\n\n\\paragraph{Dataset scale and diversity.}\nThe training set contains 500 synthetic conversations generated by a single model (Qwen3-8B), and all multi-turn OOD evaluation data is also Qwen3-8B-generated. This scale limits statistical power: the perturbation evaluation ($n$=76) means tier boundaries in Table~\\ref{tab:perturbation} correspond to 1--3 misclassifications. While ToxicChat (human-authored, single-turn) confirms that DAPT's detection advantage transfers across data sources (+18--42\\,pp over vanilla; Section~\\ref{sec:human_data}), multi-turn generalization across LLM generation sources remains to be verified. Whether the full evidence hierarchy, including classification supervision's within-distribution stabilization, generalizes to human-generated multi-turn attacks is open.\nCross-LLM validation with Llama-3.1-8B-Instruct as the target model reveals that jailbreak detection transfers across LLMs (F1 recovers to 0.80--1.00 when the benign distribution is held constant), but the benign baseline is LLM-specific: replacing Qwen benign text with Llama benign text causes false-positive rates to spike (F1 drops to 0.37--0.43). A per-class error decomposition (Appendix~\\ref{app:cross_llm}, Table~\\ref{tab:cross_llm_error}) confirms that true positive rates are identical across benign conditions; the F1 collapse is driven entirely by false positives on unfamiliar benign text. The residual cross-LLM degradation on structurally complex attacks (ActorAttack F1 = 0.80 vs.\\ 0.997 in-distribution) indicates that some jailbreak features remain source-specific. Multi-LLM training data is a clear direction for future work (Appendix~\\ref{app:cross_llm}).\n\n\\paragraph{Lexical separability.}\nTF-IDF logistic regression achieves F1 = 0.961 on OOD Deceptive Delight with near-perfect per-prefix performance; this indicates substantial lexical separability on this attack family. On DD alone, we cannot distinguish whether continued pretraining captures beyond-lexical features or exploits the same lexical cues. ActorAttack resolves this question: TF-IDF collapses to F1 = 0.261 while domain-adapted models maintain F1 $\\geq$ 0.89, a $\\geq$63\\,pp gap that demonstrates continued pretraining captures features beyond lexical overlap on structurally complex attacks. Whether this beyond-lexical advantage extends to other structurally complex attack families beyond ActorAttack requires further evaluation.\n\n\\paragraph{Strategy annotation quality.}\nPersuasion strategy labels are produced by Qwen3-8B with structured prompting. Inter-annotator agreement with a human sample ($n$=50) yields Cohen's $\\kappa$=0.78, which indicates substantial but imperfect agreement. The small human validation sample and reliance on a single annotator model limit confidence in label quality. We plan to release the annotation prompts and data to enable independent verification.\n\n\\paragraph{OOD strategy prediction.}\nWhile the 9-class model achieves high detection F1 on OOD attacks (0.997$\\pm$0.005 on Deceptive Delight), its strategy-level predictions do not generalize: OOD strategy accuracy is 30.7\\% ($\\kappa$=0.126). The interpretability provided by the persuasion taxonomy is currently limited to known attack families where strategy labels are reliable.\n\n\\paragraph{FITD discriminative power.}\nFITD provides minimal discriminative signal: both vanilla and 9-class achieve F1 $\\geq$ 0.990 at all prefixes. FITD serves primarily as a confirmation that simple OOD attacks do not challenge any variant, rather than as a meaningful evaluation axis.\n\n\\paragraph{Scope.}\nOnly persuasion-mediated multi-turn attacks fall within scope. Single-turn token-level attacks~\\citep{zou2023gcg}, non-persuasive multi-turn strategies, and attacks targeting modalities other than text are not addressed. The user-only input assumption (excluding assistant responses) may miss information in attacks where the model's own responses contribute to the escalation trajectory.\n\n\\paragraph{Computation cost.}\nStage~1 continued pretraining requires approximately 30 minutes on a single A100 GPU per variant. Stage~2 training completes in under 5 minutes. Total pipeline cost for all seven variants is under 4 GPU-hours, making the approach accessible for replication.\n",

[Director 指令] [Patch] Table 3 RoBERTa-large caption + std 修正

Reviewer 发现两个问题：

1. **Caption 错误**：写了 "RoBERTa-large: single seed" 但实际是 3-seed mean。改为正确的 "3-seed mean±std"（与其他行一致）。

2. **缺少 std**：RoBERTa-large 行需要补充 ±std，尤其是：
   - vanilla DD: .927±.036
   - vanilla AA: 1.000±.000
   - 9class DD: .987±.004
   - 9class AA: .946±.057（这个 std 不可忽略，seed 123 崩到 0.866）

格式与表中其他行统一。编译验证。

"A control experiment with halved learning rate (1$\times$10$^{-5}$) produces an even larger regression (AA Full F1 = 0.781 vs.\ 0.946; Appendix~\ref{app:roberta_large_lr}), confirming that the capacity-DAPT interaction is structural rather than hyperparameter-dependent."

在 cross-LLM validation section 之后加：

\subsection{RoBERTa-large DAPT Learning Rate Control}

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_01PKLsMcTY8pN7WM1cc9RjHU`</summary>

```text
173	
174	RoBERTa-large (355M) reveals a capacity-DAPT interaction: while domain adaptation improves DD detection across all scales (+6\,pp for large, +69\,pp for DeBERTa), its effect on ActorAttack reverses for the largest encoder (vanilla 1.000 vs.\ 9-class 0.946). Smaller encoders (125--184M) require DAPT to bridge a 47--71\,pp gap on ActorAttack. DAPT hyperparameters were not tuned for RoBERTa-large (identical learning rate and epoch count to base models), so the observed regression may partly reflect suboptimal adaptation.
175	
176	Three encoder scales thus reveal a capacity gradient for the \emph{difficulty threshold}: DeBERTa-v3-base requires adaptation for all unseen attacks, RoBERTa-base tolerates simple attacks but fails on complex ones, and RoBERTa-large handles both without adaptation. Although pretraining objectives also differ (RTD vs.\ MLM), the consistent monotonic pattern suggests model size is the dominant factor (Section~\ref{sec:discussion}).
177	Classification supervision provides a consistent stability benefit across both base encoders (cross-seed variance: 0.005--0.046 vs.\ 0.026--0.201).
178	
179	CKA analysis~\citep{kornblith2019cka} and linear probing confirm that DAPT reshapes representations substantially, though comparable CKA shifts yield different detection gains across starting points (Appendix~\ref{sec:cka_heatmap}).
180	
181	%----------------------------------------------------------------------
182	\subsection{Human Data Validation}
```

```text
233	\end{tabular}
234	\end{table}
235	
236	Table~\ref{tab:cross_llm_error} decomposes DD Full F1 into true positive and false positive rates. TP rates are identical regardless of the benign source: domain-adapted models detect Llama-generated jailbreaks at the same rate whether evaluated against Qwen or Llama benign text. The F1 collapse in the full Llama condition (Table~\ref{tab:cross_llm}, row~2) is driven entirely by false positives on unfamiliar benign text (FP rate: 88--90\% on Llama vs.\ 0--0.9\% on Qwen for adapted variants). The model implicitly treats non-Qwen text style as a jailbreak indicator, a spurious correlation from single-LLM training data. This failure reflects a data diversity limitation, not a methodological one; multi-LLM training data would address this confound directly.
237	
238	\section{Reproducibility Details}
239	\label{sec:reproducibility}
240	
241	\paragraph{Stage-1 training.}
242	All variants use DeBERTa-v3-base (184M parameters) with learning rate $2 \times 10^{-5}$, AdamW optimizer ($\beta_1$=0.9, $\beta_2$=0.999, weight decay 0.01), linear warmup over the first 10\% of steps, batch size 16, and 4 epochs. MLM variants use 15\% random token masking. Training takes approximately 30 minutes per variant on a single NVIDIA A100 (40GB).
```

          "+RoBERTa-large (355M) reveals a capacity-DAPT interaction: while domain adaptation improves DD detection across all scales (+6\\,pp for large, +69\\,pp for DeBERTa), its effect on ActorAttack reverses for the largest encoder (vanilla 1.000 vs.\\ 9-class 0.946).           " ",
          " Three encoder scales thus reveal a capacity gradient for the \\emph{difficulty threshold}: DeBERTa-v3-base requires adaptation for all unseen attacks, RoBERTa-base tolerates simple attacks but fails on complex ones, and RoBERTa-large handles both without adaptation.   "content": "[Director 指令] [Patch] 叙事重构 — 从 descriptive 升级为 narrative-driven（D044）\n\nReviewer 深度评估指出论文当前偏描述性（\"we tested X, found Y\"），缺乏叙事张力。DDL 还有 6 天，以下修改零 GPU 成本但预期提分显著。\n\n### 修改 1: Abstract 锐化\n\n当前 abstract 是描述性的（\"three findings emerge\"）。需要更强的 hook。方向：\n\n开头制造 tension：\"Multi-turn jailbreak detection requires models to track escalating manipulation across conversation turns, yet how pretrained encoders acquire this capability remains unclear.\"\n\n中间强调 reductive insight：\"Through systematic ablation of six pretraining dimensions across twelve encoder variants, we arrive at a reductive answer: for base-size encoders (125--184M parameters), domain exposure alone bridges the OOD gap; label quality, temporal modeling, and classification supervision itself are non-factors.\"\n\n结尾收束 capacity gradient：\"Scaling to 355M parameters eliminates the need for domain adaptation entirely on structurally distinct attacks, revealing a capacity gradient that determines when adaptation is critical.\"\n\n**注意**：保持 150-250 words 限制。不过度宣传（no \"groundbreaking\"/\"revolutionary\"）。保留具体数字支撑 claim。\n\n### 修改 2: Introduction 叙事张力\n\n在 Introduction 的问题切入段（现有的 vanilla collapse + LLM judge fail 段落），让 tension 更锐利：\n\n- 明确写出\"naive hypothesis\"然后打破它：\"The simplest hypothesis, that pretrained language understanding transfers directly to jailbreak detection, fails: vanilla DeBERTa-v3 collapses to F1 = 0.31 on out-of-distribution attacks.\"\n- 快速建立 stakes\n\n### 修改 3: §4.2 或 §4.3 末尾统一 null result summary\n\n在 decomposition 分析结束后加 1 段收束（~3-4 句）：\n\n\"Of the six pretraining dimensions we decomposed, only domain exposure significantly affects cross-attack detection. Classification supervision, label correctness, label granularity, temporal modeling, and training-domain specificity are all non-factors within the synthetic distribution (Table~\\ref{tab:main_results}). This reductive finding has practical implications: practitioners need only continued pretraining on domain text, not expensive annotation pipelines or specialized architectures.\"\n\n### 不做的事：\n- **不改 title**——当前 question form 对 EMNLP 合适，改 assertive title 风险太大\n- **不加新 figure**——8 页已满，summary figure 放 Appendix 也不划算\n- **不改 Claim 3 措辞**——当前表述安全，强化措辞风险不对等\n\n不用 trailing -ing phrases，不用 em-dash，不用黑名单词。编译验证 0 errors，主文 ≤ 8 页。"
}

[Director 指令] [Patch] 叙事重构 — 从 descriptive 升级为 narrative-driven（D044）

Reviewer 深度评估指出论文当前偏描述性（"we tested X, found Y"），缺乏叙事张力。DDL 还有 6 天，以下修改零 GPU 成本但预期提分显著。

### 修改 1: Abstract 锐化

当前 abstract 是描述性的（"three findings emerge"）。需要更强的 hook。方向：

开头制造 tension："Multi-turn jailbreak detection requires models to track escalating manipulation across conversation turns, yet how pretrained encoders acquire this capability remains unclear."

中间强调 reductive insight："Through systematic ablation of six pretraining dimensions across twelve encoder variants, we arrive at a reductive answer: for base-size encoders (125--184M parameters), domain exposure alone bridges the OOD gap; label quality, temporal modeling, and classification supervision itself are non-factors."

结尾收束 capacity gradient："Scaling to 355M parameters eliminates the need for domain adaptation entirely on structurally distinct attacks, revealing a capacity gradient that determines when adaptation is critical."

**注意**：保持 150-250 words 限制。不过度宣传（no "groundbreaking"/"revolutionary"）。保留具体数字支撑 claim。

### 修改 2: Introduction 叙事张力

在 Introduction 的问题切入段（现有的 vanilla collapse + LLM judge fail 段落），让 tension 更锐利：

- 明确写出"naive hypothesis"然后打破它："The simplest hypothesis, that pretrained language understanding transfers directly to jailbreak detection, fails: vanilla DeBERTa-v3 collapses to F1 = 0.31 on out-of-distribution attacks."
- 快速建立 stakes

### 修改 3: §4.2 或 §4.3 末尾统一 null result summary

在 decomposition 分析结束后加 1 段收束（~3-4 句）：

"Of the six pretraining dimensions we decomposed, only domain exposure significantly affects cross-attack detection. Classification supervision, label correctness, label granularity, temporal modeling, and training-domain specificity are all non-factors within the synthetic distribution (Table~\ref{tab:main_results}). This reductive finding has practical implications: practitioners need only continued pretraining on domain text, not expensive annotation pipelines or specialized architectures."

- **不改 Claim 3 措辞**——当前表述安全，强化措辞风险不对等

```text
1	% introduction.tex — expanded for 8-page target
2	
3	\section{Introduction}
4	\label{sec:introduction}
5	
6	Multi-turn jailbreak attacks pose a qualitatively different threat to large language model (LLM) safety than their single-turn counterparts. Attacks such as Crescendo~\citep{russinovich2024crescendo}, Foot-in-the-Door (FITD)~\citep{fitd2024}, and Deceptive Delight~\citep{deceptivedelight2024} distribute adversarial intent across individually benign turns through rapport building, logical reframing, and incremental escalation. Persuasion is the common mechanism: \citet{zeng2024pap} catalogue 40 persuasion techniques across jailbreak families, and \citet{hagendorff2026reasoning} find identifiable strategies in 97\% of successful attacks. Individual attack turns occupy the same embedding region as benign text~\citep{bullwinkel2025repeng}; per-turn safety filters are fundamentally blind to such attacks.
7	
8	Production classifiers such as Llama Guard~\citep{inan2023llamaguard}, WildGuard~\citep{han2024wildguard}, and ShieldGemma~\citep{zeng2024shieldgemma} operate on individual turns and cannot model manipulation trajectories across exchanges. Recent multi-turn defenses have begun to address conversation-level structure: DeepContext~\citep{deepcontext2026} combines fine-tuned BERT with a multi-layer GRU, BIID~\citep{tong2025biid} infers turn-level intentions, and G-Guard~\citep{gguard2025} applies graph neural networks. These systems achieve reasonable in-distribution performance but are evaluated on single attack families and do not assess cross-dataset OOD transfer. None systematically analyze \emph{why} some representations generalize while others fail. The open question is: \emph{what representation properties enable cross-attack generalization?}
9	
10	A vanilla DeBERTa encoder~\citep{he2021deberta} with temporal aggregation, representing the class of pretrained-encoder-plus-sequence-model approaches without domain adaptation (inspired by but not directly replicating DeepContext~\citep{deepcontext2026}, which differs in encoder fine-tuning and loss function), collapses from F1\,=\,1.000 on in-distribution data to F1\,=\,0.312$\pm$0.026 on out-of-distribution (OOD) Deceptive Delight, worsening with more turns (0.628 at turn~1 to 0.312 at full conversation). LLM-as-judge approaches fare no better: GPT-5.5 achieves F1 of only 0.191--0.449 on in-distribution detection with 30--47\% unparseable responses (Appendix~\ref{sec:llm_judge}). Content-level reasoning is insufficient for this task.
11	
12	We address this question through a controlled ablation study. Using a two-stage pipeline (encoder adaptation followed by frozen BiGRU classification, which we call \textbf{DisPeD}), we design seven Stage~1 encoder variants: 9-class persuasion strategy classification, binary classification, scrambled labels, MLM only, topic classification, Wikipedia MLM, and vanilla control (Section~\ref{sec:method}). Each variant isolates a specific factor (domain exposure, classification supervision, label quality, label granularity, or domain specificity) while holding all other components constant. The core contribution is the systematic decomposition of what enables cross-attack generalization, not the detection pipeline itself.
13	
14	Three principal findings emerge from ablation across DeBERTa-v3 and RoBERTa~\citep{liu2019roberta} on synthetic multi-turn data spanning four attack families. For base-size encoders (125--184M), domain exposure via DAPT is the primary mechanism, bridging $\sim$60\,pp of the OOD gap without labels; lexical baselines collapse on structurally complex attacks regardless (TF-IDF F1: 0.261 on ActorAttack). Three encoder scales (125M, 184M, 355M) reveal a capacity gradient: as model size increases, the set of attacks requiring domain adaptation narrows, with the largest encoder (RoBERTa-large, 355M) achieving F1\,=\,1.000 on ActorAttack~\citep{ren2024actorattack} without adaptation. Classification supervision stabilizes predictions within the synthetic distribution (std reduced 4--20$\times$) regardless of label correctness, but does not transfer to human-authored data, where all classification variants underperform pure MLM.
15	
16	Our contributions:
17	\begin{itemize}[leftmargin=*, itemsep=2pt, topsep=4pt]
18	  \item \textbf{Systematic decomposition of cross-attack generalization.} We decompose six dimensions of encoder adaptation (domain exposure, classification supervision, label quality, label granularity, domain specificity, and temporal modeling) through controlled ablation across twelve encoder variants and three OOD attack families. For base-size encoders (125--184M), domain-adaptive pretraining is the primary mechanism ($\sim$60\,pp F1 gap); for the largest encoder (355M), pretrained representations already suffice on structurally complex attacks. Classification supervision, label quality, and temporal structure are non-factors for detection, yielding several counter-intuitive negative results.
19	
20	  \item \textbf{Capacity gradient for domain adaptation.} Three encoder scales (125M, 184M, 355M) reveal a clear capacity gradient: as pretrained model size increases, the set of attacks requiring domain adaptation shrinks. DeBERTa-v3-base requires adaptation for all unseen attacks, RoBERTa-base tolerates structurally simple attacks but fails on complex ones, and RoBERTa-large handles both attack types without adaptation. Although pretraining objectives also differ (RTD vs.\ MLM), the monotonic pattern across three scales suggests model size is the primary factor (Section~\ref{sec:cross_encoder}).
21	
22	  \item \textbf{Distribution-specific classification effects.} All classification-supervised variants achieve equivalent detection regardless of label correctness (F1 gap $\leq$3.9\,pp), with 4--20$\times$ cross-seed variance reduction on synthetic OOD evaluations vs.\ MLM-only. This stability benefit does not transfer to human-authored data (Section~\ref{sec:human_data}). The 9-class taxonomy provides interpretable strategy characterization for known attack families (Section~\ref{sec:strategy}).
23	\end{itemize}
24	
```

```text
1	% abstract.tex — R3 revision
2	\begin{abstract}
3	Multi-turn jailbreak attacks distribute adversarial intent across seemingly benign conversational turns, evading per-turn safety filters.
4	What representation properties enable detection that generalizes across attack families?
5	We address this through a controlled ablation on synthetic multi-turn evaluation data: seven encoder variants on DeBERTa-v3, three on RoBERTa-base, and two on RoBERTa-large (355M), evaluated across three synthetic out-of-distribution (OOD) attack families plus in-distribution Crescendo, decompose the roles of domain exposure, classification supervision, label quality, and domain specificity.
6	Three findings emerge.
7	First, for base-size encoders (125--184M), domain exposure via DAPT bridges $\sim$60 percentage points of the OOD gap without labels, though the largest encoder tested (355M) achieves strong detection without adaptation on structurally complex attacks.
8	Second, three encoder scales (125M, 184M, 355M) reveal a capacity gradient: as model size increases, the set of attacks requiring domain adaptation narrows.
9	Third, classification supervision stabilizes predictions within the synthetic training distribution (variance reduced 4--20$\times$ on synthetic OOD) regardless of label correctness, but this benefit does not transfer to human-authored data, where all classification variants underperform pure MLM.
10	DAPT on synthetic text transfers to human-written ToxicChat jailbreaks (macro F1: 0.837 vs.\ vanilla 0.413); full multi-turn validation on human data remains future work.
11	\end{abstract}
12

72	Figure~\ref{fig:cross_attack_bar} visualizes the prefix-length trajectory on OOD DD.
73	The vanilla model's monotonic degradation from $K$=1 (0.628$\pm$0.038) to full conversation (0.312$\pm$0.026) confirms that additional attack turns, each individually benign-appearing, progressively confuse a non-adapted encoder.
74	MLM's strong early performance ($K$=2: 0.993) followed by instability at full length (0.914$\pm$0.115) suggests that domain exposure enables early detection of persuasion markers, but longer conversations introduce distributional drift that unsupervised pretraining cannot fully accommodate.
75	The 9-class model's flat trajectory (0.972--0.997 across all prefixes) demonstrates that classification supervision stabilizes representations against this drift.
76	
77	%----------------------------------------------------------------------
78	\subsection{Decomposing the Detection Mechanism}
79	\label{sec:decomposition}
80	
81	\begin{table}[t]
82	\centering
83	\small
84	\caption{Evidence hierarchy on OOD DD and ActorAttack (AA) at full conversation length (3-seed mean$\pm$std where available). All variants share the same Stage-2 pipeline. Full per-prefix results in Appendix Tables~\ref{tab:full_prefix} and~\ref{tab:actorattack_full}.}
85	\label{tab:evidence_hierarchy}
86	\begin{tabular}{@{}lccc@{}}
87	\toprule
88	\textbf{Variant} & DD-Full & AA-Full & DD-$K$=2 \\
89	\midrule
90	9-class (full model) & .997$\pm$.005 & .972$\pm$.026 & .981$\pm$.013 \\
91	Binary (2-class) & 1.000$\pm$.000 & .990$\pm$.008 & \textbf{.994$\pm$.009} \\
92	Scrambled labels & .997$\pm$.005 & .951$\pm$.043 & .981$\pm$.026 \\
93	MLM-only (no labels) & .914$\pm$.115 & .893$\pm$.080 & .993$\pm$.009 \\
94	Mean pooling$^{*}$ & .991$\pm$.013 & .962$\pm$.015 & 1.000 \\
95	\midrule
96	Topic classif.\ (5-class) & .541$\pm$.207 & .625$\pm$.141 & .593$\pm$.166 \\
97	Wikipedia MLM & .904$\pm$.115 & .686$\pm$.297 & .911$\pm$.073 \\
98	Vanilla DeBERTa & .312$\pm$.026 & .258$\pm$.000 & .479$\pm$.038 \\
99	TF-IDF LR$^{\dagger}$ & .961 & .261 & 1.000 \\
100	\bottomrule
101	\end{tabular}
102	\vspace{-2pt}
103	{\footnotesize $^{*}$9-class encoder with mean pooling replacing BiGRU. $^{\dagger}$Deterministic (no std).}
104	\end{table}
105	
106	Table~\ref{tab:evidence_hierarchy} reports F1 for all variants on DD and ActorAttack. The \emph{evidence hierarchy} (ordering of variants by OOD detection performance) at full conversation length on DD is:
107	
108	\vspace{2pt}
109	\noindent\hspace{0.5em}{\small vanilla (0.31$\pm$0.03) $<$ topic (0.54$\pm$0.21) $\ll$ wiki-MLM (0.90$\pm$0.12) $\approx$ jb-MLM (0.91$\pm$0.12) $<$ scrambled (0.997$\pm$0.005) $=$ 9-class (0.997$\pm$0.005) $\approx$ binary (1.000$\pm$0.000)}
110	\vspace{2pt}
111	
112	\paragraph{Domain exposure is the primary mechanism.}
113	MLM bridges $\sim$60\,pp from vanilla (0.312$\pm$0.026 $\to$ 0.914$\pm$0.115) without labels; this supports the DAPT interpretation~\citep{gururangan2020dontstop}. TF-IDF collapse on ActorAttack (0.261 vs.\ 9-class 0.972) confirms that domain-adapted representations capture features beyond lexical overlap.
114	All three classification-supervised variants converge to equivalent detection (F1 = 0.997--1.000); McNemar's test finds no significant pairwise differences (all $p > 0.13$; $\leq$7 discordant pairs limit power), and paired permutation tests confirm equivalence ($p \geq 0.40$). TOST equivalence tests (margin = 5\,pp) confirm statistical equivalence on DD (all $p < 0.01$, 90\% CI within $\pm$0.05). On ActorAttack, equivalence cannot be statistically confirmed due to limited seeds ($n$=3), though observed mean differences (2--4\,pp) fall within practical equivalence margins. Label quality and granularity are non-factors for detection.
115	Classification supervision reduces cross-seed variance 4--20$\times$ over MLM on synthetic OOD (std 0.005 vs.\ 0.115 on DD; bootstrap CI widths: 5.7\,pp vs.\ 35.5\,pp). This stability benefit does not transfer to human-authored data, where classification-supervised variants exhibit the highest variance and pure MLM the lowest (Section~\ref{sec:human_data}).
116	Appendix~\ref{app:tsne} provides t-SNE visualizations confirming that domain-adapted variants produce tighter, more separable embedding clusters than the vanilla baseline.
117	
118	\paragraph{Domain specificity is marginal at full length but meaningful for early detection.}
119	Wikipedia MLM (0.904$\pm$0.115) nearly matches jailbreak-domain MLM at full length (1\,pp gap); general continued pretraining provides most of the representational benefit.
120	At early prefixes, jailbreak-domain text provides an 8\,pp advantage ($K$=2: 0.993 vs.\ 0.911), consistent with the interpretation that domain-specific features (persuasion markers, role-play framing) are most informative when the model has seen fewer turns.
121	As conversations grow longer, generic linguistic features accumulated through any continued pretraining become sufficient for discrimination.
122	Perturbation analysis (Section~\ref{sec:perturbation}) reveals a sharper domain specificity effect: Wikipedia MLM degrades by $\sim$12\,pp under adversarial paraphrasing while jailbreak-domain MLM degrades by only 2.6\,pp.
123	
124	\paragraph{Misaligned supervision destabilizes generalization.}
125	Topic classification yields DD-Full F1 = 0.541$\pm$0.207; seed 123 falls \emph{below} vanilla (0.252 vs.\ 0.312). Despite using the same jailbreak-domain training data, topic classification performs worse than Wikipedia MLM (0.904$\pm$0.115), which uses out-of-domain text with no labels. A misaligned objective forces the encoder to allocate capacity to irrelevant features and actively destabilizes representations that MLM would preserve.
126	
127	\paragraph{Evidence hierarchy on structurally complex attacks.}
128	ActorAttack~\citep{ren2024actorattack} uses multi-persona steering across longer conversation structures and provides a more challenging OOD benchmark than DD.
129	The evidence hierarchy at full conversation length is:
130	
131	\vspace{2pt}
132	\noindent\hspace{0.5em}{\small TF-IDF (0.26) $\approx$ vanilla (0.26$\pm$0.00) $<$ topic (0.63$\pm$0.14) $<$ wiki-MLM (0.69$\pm$0.30) $<$ jb-MLM (0.89$\pm$0.08) $<$ scrambled (0.95$\pm$0.04) $\approx$ 9-class (0.97$\pm$0.03) $\approx$ binary (0.99$\pm$0.01)}
133	\vspace{2pt}
134	
135	TF-IDF collapses from 0.961 (DD) to 0.261 (ActorAttack, precision = 1.0, recall = 0.15). This gap shows that domain-adapted representations capture features beyond bag-of-words.
136	All classification-supervised variants converge to equivalent performance (0.951--0.990, max gap 3.9\,pp); this extends the DD finding. DAPT provides $\sim$71\,pp over both vanilla (0.258) and TF-IDF (0.261). Full per-prefix results in Appendix~\ref{sec:actorattack_full}.
137	
138	\paragraph{Temporal modeling.}
139	Three-seed evaluation on both DD and ActorAttack confirms that mean pooling matches BiGRU (F1 gap $\leq$ 1\,pp on full conversations, overlapping CIs); detection thus relies on per-turn representations rather than their sequential order.
140

\begin{abstract}
Multi-turn jailbreak detection requires models to track escalating manipulation across conversation turns, yet how pretrained encoders acquire this capability remains unclear.
We decompose this question through controlled ablation: twelve encoder variants spanning three scales (125M, 184M, 355M) and six pretraining dimensions, evaluated across three synthetic out-of-distribution (OOD) attack families plus in-distribution Crescendo.


```

The findings are surprisingly stark: smaller encoders rely almost entirely on domain exposure to close the OOD gap—label quality, granularity, and temporal modeling barely matter—but scaling to 355M parameters makes domain adaptation unnecessary for structurally different attacks, suggesting a capacity threshold where the model's inherent robustness takes over. Classification supervision does stabilize predictions within the training distribution, but this benefit doesn't generalize to real human-authored attacks.

However, domain-adaptive pretraining on synthetic text does transfer to human jailbreaks in ToxicChat, improving performance from 0.413 to 0.837 F1, though validating across full multi-turn conversations remains an open question.

```

  "old_string": "% abstract.tex — R3 revision\n\\begin{abstract}\nMulti-turn jailbreak attacks distribute adversarial intent across seemingly benign conversational turns, evading per-turn safety filters.\nWhat representation properties enable detection that generalizes across attack families?\nWe address this through a controlled ablation on synthetic multi-turn evaluation data: seven encoder variants on DeBERTa-v3, three on RoBERTa-base, and two on RoBERTa-large (355M), evaluated across three synthetic out-of-distribution (OOD) attack families plus in-distribution Crescendo, decompose the roles of domain exposure, classification supervision, label quality, and domain specificity.\nThree findings emerge.\nFirst, for base-size encoders (125--184M), domain exposure via DAPT bridges $\\sim$60 percentage points of the OOD gap without labels, though the largest encoder tested (355M) achieves strong detection without adaptation on structurally complex attacks.\nSecond, three encoder scales (125M, 184M, 355M) reveal a capacity gradient: as model size increases, the set of attacks requiring domain adaptation narrows.\nThird, classification supervision stabilizes predictions within the synthetic training distribution (variance reduced 4--20$\\times$ on synthetic OOD) regardless of label correctness, but this benefit does not transfer to human-authored data, where all classification variants underperform pure MLM.\nDAPT on synthetic text transfers to human-written ToxicChat jailbreaks (macro F1: 0.837 vs.\\ vanilla 0.413); full multi-turn validation on human data remains future work.\n\\end{abstract}",
  "new_string": "% abstract.tex — D044 narrative revision\n\\begin{abstract}\nMulti-turn jailbreak detection requires models to track escalating manipulation across conversation turns, yet how pretrained encoders acquire this capability remains unclear.\nWe decompose this question through controlled ablation: twelve encoder variants spanning three scales (125M, 184M, 355M) and six pretraining dimensions, evaluated across three synthetic out-of-distribution (OOD) attack families plus in-distribution Crescendo.\nThe answer is reductive.\nFor base-size encoders (125--184M parameters), domain exposure alone bridges $\\sim$60 percentage points of the OOD gap; label quality, label granularity, temporal modeling, and classification supervision itself are non-factors for detection.\nScaling to 355M parameters eliminates the need for domain adaptation entirely on structurally distinct attacks (F1\\,=\\,1.000 on ActorAttack without adaptation), revealing a capacity gradient that determines when adaptation is critical.\nClassification supervision stabilizes predictions within the synthetic training distribution (variance reduced 4--20$\\times$) regardless of label correctness, but this benefit does not transfer to human-authored data, where all classification variants underperform pure MLM.\nDomain-adaptive pretraining (DAPT) on synthetic text transfers to human-written ToxicChat jailbreaks (macro F1: 0.837 vs.\\ vanilla 0.413); full multi-turn validation on human data remains future work.\n\\end{abstract}"
}

Content-level reasoning is insufficient.",
    "originalFile": "% introduction.tex — expanded for 8-page target\n\n\\section{Introduction}\n\\label{sec:introduction}\n\nMulti-turn jailbreak attacks pose a qualitatively different threat to large language model (LLM) safety than their single-turn counterparts. Attacks such as Crescendo~\\citep{russinovich2024crescendo}, Foot-in-the-Door (FITD)~\\citep{fitd2024}, and Deceptive Delight~\\citep{deceptivedelight2024} distribute adversarial intent across individually benign turns through rapport building, logical reframing, and incremental escalation. Persuasion is the common mechanism: \\citet{zeng2024pap} catalogue 40 persuasion techniques across jailbreak families, and \\citet{hagendorff2026reasoning} find identifiable strategies in 97\\% of successful attacks. Individual attack turns occupy the same embedding region as benign text~\\citep{bullwinkel2025repeng}; per-turn safety filters are fundamentally blind to such attacks.\n\nProduction classifiers such as Llama Guard~\\citep{inan2023llamaguard}, WildGuard~\\citep{han2024wildguard}, and ShieldGemma~\\citep{zeng2024shieldgemma} operate on individual turns and cannot model manipulation trajectories across exchanges. Recent multi-turn defenses have begun to address conversation-level structure: DeepContext~\\citep{deepcontext2026} combines fine-tuned BERT with a multi-layer GRU, BIID~\\citep{tong2025biid} infers turn-level intentions, and G-Guard~\\citep{gguard2025} applies graph neural networks. These systems achieve reasonable in-distribution performance but are evaluated on single attack families and do not assess cross-dataset OOD transfer. None systematically analyze \\emph{why} some representations generalize while others fail. The open question is: \\emph{what representation properties enable cross-attack generalization?}\n\nA vanilla DeBERTa encoder~\\citep{he2021deberta} with temporal aggregation, representing the class of pretrained-encoder-plus-sequence-model approaches without domain adaptation (inspired by but not directly replicating DeepContext~\\citep{deepcontext2026}, which differs in encoder fine-tuning and loss function), collapses from F1\\,=\\,1.000 on in-distribution data to F1\\,=\\,0.312$\\pm$0.026 on out-of-distribution (OOD) Deceptive Delight, worsening with more turns (0.628 at turn~1 to 0.312 at full conversation). LLM-as-judge approaches fare no better: GPT-5.5 achieves F1 of only 0.191--0.449 on in-distribution detection with 30--47\\% unparseable responses (Appendix~\\ref{sec:llm_judge}). Content-level reasoning is insufficient for this task.\n\nWe address this question through a controlled ablation study. Using a two-stage pipeline (encoder adaptation followed by frozen BiGRU classification, which we call \\textbf{DisPeD}), we design seven Stage~1 encoder variants: 9-class persuasion strategy classification, binary classification, scrambled labels, MLM only, topic classification, Wikipedia MLM, and vanilla control (Section~\\ref{sec:method}). Each variant isolates a specific factor (domain exposure, classification supervision, label quality, label granularity, or domain specificity) while holding all other components constant. The core contribution is the systematic decomposition of what enables cross-attack generalization, not the detection pipeline itself.\n\nThree principal findings emerge from ablation across DeBERTa-v3 and RoBERTa~\\citep{liu2019roberta} on synthetic multi-turn data spanning four attack families. For base-size encoders (125--184M), domain exposure via DAPT is the primary mechanism, bridging $\\sim$60\\,pp of the OOD gap without labels; lexical baselines collapse on structurally complex attacks regardless (TF-IDF F1: 0.261 on ActorAttack). Three encoder scales (125M, 184M, 355M) reveal a capacity gradient: as model size increases, the set of attacks requiring domain adaptation narrows, with the largest encoder (RoBERTa-large, 355M) achieving F1\\,=\\,1.000 on ActorAttack~\\citep{ren2024actorattack} without adaptation. Classification supervision stabilizes predictions within the synthetic distribution (std reduced 4--20$\\times$) regardless of label correctness, but does not transfer to human-authored data, where all classification variants underperform pure MLM.\n\nOur contributions:\n\\begin{itemize}[leftmargin=*, itemsep=2pt, topsep=4pt]\n  \\item \\textbf{Systematic decomposition of cross-attack generalization.} We decompose six dimensions of encoder adaptation (domain exposure, classification supervision, label quality, label granularity, domain specificity, and temporal modeling) through controlled ablation across twelve encoder variants and three OOD attack families. For base-size encoders (125--184M), domain-adaptive pretraining is the primary mechanism ($\\sim$60\\,pp F1 gap); for the largest encoder (355M), pretrained representations already suffice on structurally complex attacks. Classification supervision, label quality, and temporal structure are non-factors for detection, yielding several counter-intuitive negative results.\n\n  \\item \\textbf{Capacity gradient for domain adaptation.} Three encoder scales (125M, 184M, 355M) reveal a clear capacity gradient: as pretrained model size increases, the set of attacks requiring domain adaptation shrinks. DeBERTa-v3-base requires adaptation for all unseen attacks, RoBERTa-base tolerates structurally simple attacks but fails on complex ones, and RoBERTa-large handles both attack types without adaptation. Although pretraining objectives also differ (RTD vs.\\ MLM), the monotonic pattern across three scales suggests model size is the primary factor (Section~\\ref{sec:cross_encoder}).\n\n  \\item \\textbf{Distribution-specific classification effects.} All classification-supervised variants achieve equivalent detection regardless of label correctness (F1 gap $\\leq$3.9\\,pp), with 4--20$\\times$ cross-seed variance reduction on synthetic OOD evaluations vs.\\ MLM-only. This stability benefit does not transfer to human-authored data (Section~\\ref{sec:human_data}). The 9-class taxonomy provides interpretable strategy characterization for known attack families (Section~\\ref{sec:strategy}).\n\\end{itemize}\n",

  "new_string": "\\paragraph{Temporal modeling.}\nThree-seed evaluation on both DD and ActorAttack confirms that mean pooling matches BiGRU (F1 gap $\\leq$ 1\\,pp on full conversations, overlapping CIs); detection thus relies on per-turn representations rather than their sequential order.\n\n\\paragraph{Summary.}\nOf the six dimensions decomposed, only domain exposure significantly affects cross-attack detection at full conversation length. Classification supervision, label correctness, label granularity, and temporal modeling are non-factors; domain specificity is marginal (1\\,pp gap at full length, though 8\\,pp at $K$=2). Practitioners need continued pretraining on domain text, not annotation pipelines or specialized temporal architectures.\n\n%----------------------------------------------------------------------\n\\subsection{Cross-Encoder Validation}"
}

Option A: Compress the summary to 2 sentences:


Of the six dimensions decomposed, only domain exposure significantly affects cross-attack detection at full conversation length; the others—classification supervision, label correctness, label granularity, temporal modeling, and domain specificity—are non-factors. Practitioners should focus on continued pretraining with domain text rather than investing in annotation pipelines or specialized temporal architectures.

This saves ~1 line. Let me also look for other compressions.

Option B: In §4.2, the "Domain specificity" paragraph is 4 sentences and could be compressed since the summary now covers the key finding. I should trim that section down to avoid redundancy.

Looking at the empirical results, general continued pretraining on Wikipedia text achieves nearly the same performance as domain-specific jailbreak pretraining at full conversation length (just 1pp gap), suggesting that generic linguistic features become sufficient for discrimination as conversations grow longer. Domain-specific features like persuasion markers and role-play framing matter most early on when the model has limited context, but this advantage diminishes substantially. The perturbation analysis shows this more starkly—Wikipedia pretraining degrades by ~12pp under adversarial paraphrasing while jailbreak-domain pretraining only drops 2.6pp, indicating the latter learns more robust representations.
```

[Director 指令] [Patch] 叙事重构 over-claim 修复 — 3 处统一修正

Reviewer 识别出 classification supervision 被错误归为 "non-factor"（实际有 8pp mean + 23x variance reduction）。以下 3 处需要修改：

### 1. abstract.tex — 移除 classification supervision 从 non-factor 列表

找到类似 "label quality, label granularity, temporal modeling, and classification supervision itself are non-factors" 的句子。

改为："label quality, label granularity, and temporal modeling are non-factors; classification supervision adds stability but the detection gap is bridged by domain exposure alone"

### 2. introduction.tex — hypothesis-breaking 句加 scope

找到 "The simplest hypothesis, that pretrained language understanding transfers directly to jailbreak detection, fails."

改为："The simplest hypothesis, that pretrained language understanding transfers directly to cross-attack detection, fails for base-size encoders."

（保持叙事张力但与 RoBERTa-large vanilla AA=1.000 数据一致）

### 3. experiments.tex §4.3 null result summary paragraph

找到类似 "only domain exposure significantly affects cross-attack detection...classification supervision...are non-factors" 的句子。

改为："Domain exposure is the primary mechanism for cross-attack detection; label correctness, label granularity, temporal modeling, and domain specificity are non-factors. Classification supervision provides a complementary stability benefit (variance reduced 4--20$\times$) but does not change the detection ceiling established by domain exposure."

不用 trailing -ing phrases，不用 em-dash。编译验证。

1	% abstract.tex — D044 narrative revision
2	\begin{abstract}
3	Multi-turn jailbreak detection requires models to track escalating manipulation across conversation turns, yet how pretrained encoders acquire this capability remains unclear.
4	We decompose this question through controlled ablation: twelve encoder variants spanning three scales (125M, 184M, 355M) and six pretraining dimensions, evaluated across three synthetic out-of-distribution (OOD) attack families plus in-distribution Crescendo.
5	The answer is reductive.
6	For base-size encoders (125--184M parameters), domain exposure alone bridges $\sim$60 percentage points of the OOD gap; label quality, label granularity, temporal modeling, and classification supervision itself are non-factors for detection.
7	Scaling to 355M parameters eliminates the need for domain adaptation entirely on structurally distinct attacks (F1\,=\,1.000 on ActorAttack without adaptation), revealing a capacity gradient that determines when adaptation is critical.
8	Classification supervision stabilizes predictions within the synthetic training distribution (variance reduced 4--20$\times$) regardless of label correctness, but this benefit does not transfer to human-authored data, where all classification variants underperform pure MLM.
9	Domain-adaptive pretraining (DAPT) on synthetic text transfers to human-written ToxicChat jailbreaks (macro F1: 0.837 vs.\ vanilla 0.413); full multi-turn validation on human data remains future work.
10	\end{abstract}
11

    "filePath": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0514_2/projects/persuasion_turn_jailbreak_detect/paper/introduction.tex",
    "oldString": "The simplest hypothesis, that pretrained language understanding transfers directly to jailbreak detection, fails.",
    "newString": "The simplest hypothesis, that pretrained language understanding transfers directly to cross-attack detection, fails for base-size encoders.",
    "originalFile": "% introduction.tex — expanded for 8-page target\n\n\\section{Introduction}\n\\label{sec:introduction}\n\nMulti-turn jailbreak attacks pose a qualitatively different threat to large language model (LLM) safety than their single-turn counterparts. Attacks such as Crescendo~\\citep{russinovich2024crescendo}, Foot-in-the-Door (FITD)~\\citep{fitd2024}, and Deceptive Delight~\\citep{deceptivedelight2024} distribute adversarial intent across individually benign turns through rapport building, logical reframing, and incremental escalation. Persuasion is the common mechanism: \\citet{zeng2024pap} catalogue 40 persuasion techniques across jailbreak families, and \\citet{hagendorff2026reasoning} find identifiable strategies in 97\\% of successful attacks. Individual attack turns occupy the same embedding region as benign text~\\citep{bullwinkel2025repeng}; per-turn safety filters are fundamentally blind to such attacks.\n\nProduction classifiers such as Llama Guard~\\citep{inan2023llamaguard}, WildGuard~\\citep{han2024wildguard}, and ShieldGemma~\\citep{zeng2024shieldgemma} operate on individual turns and cannot model manipulation trajectories across exchanges. Recent multi-turn defenses have begun to address conversation-level structure: DeepContext~\\citep{deepcontext2026} combines fine-tuned BERT with a multi-layer GRU, BIID~\\citep{tong2025biid} infers turn-level intentions, and G-Guard~\\citep{gguard2025} applies graph neural networks. These systems achieve reasonable in-distribution performance but are evaluated on single attack families and do not assess cross-dataset OOD transfer. None systematically analyze \\emph{why} some representations generalize while others fail. The open question is: \\emph{what representation properties enable cross-attack generalization?}\n\nThe simplest hypothesis, that pretrained language understanding transfers directly to jailbreak detection, fails. A vanilla DeBERTa-v3 encoder~\\citep{he2021deberta} with temporal aggregation, representing pretrained-encoder-plus-sequence-model approaches without domain adaptation (inspired by but not directly replicating DeepContext~\\citep{deepcontext2026}, which differs in encoder fine-tuning and loss function), collapses from F1\\,=\\,1.000 on in-distribution data to F1\\,=\\,0.312$\\pm$0.026 on out-of-distribution (OOD) Deceptive Delight; the gap widens with more turns (0.628 at turn~1, 0.312 at full conversation). LLM-as-judge approaches fare no better: GPT-5.5 achieves F1 of only 0.191--0.449 on in-distribution detection with 30--47\\% unparseable responses (Appendix~\\ref{sec:llm_judge}). Content-level reasoning is insufficient.\n\nWe address this question through a controlled ablation study. Using a two-stage pipeline (encoder adaptation followed by frozen BiGRU classification, which we call \\textbf{DisPeD}), we design seven Stage~1 encoder variants: 9-class persuasion strategy classification, binary classification, scrambled labels, MLM only, topic classification, Wikipedia MLM, and vanilla control (Section~\\ref{sec:method}). Each variant isolates a specific factor (domain exposure, classification supervision, label quality, label granularity, or domain specificity) while holding all other components constant. The core contribution is the systematic decomposition of what enables cross-attack generalization, not the detection pipeline itself.\n\nThree principal findings emerge from ablation across DeBERTa-v3 and RoBERTa~\\citep{liu2019roberta} on synthetic multi-turn data spanning four attack families. For base-size encoders (125--184M), domain exposure via DAPT is the primary mechanism, bridging $\\sim$60\\,pp of the OOD gap without labels; lexical baselines collapse on structurally complex attacks regardless (TF-IDF F1: 0.261 on ActorAttack). Three encoder scales (125M, 184M, 355M) reveal a capacity gradient: as model size increases, the set of attacks requiring domain adaptation narrows, with the largest encoder (RoBERTa-large, 355M) achieving F1\\,=\\,1.000 on ActorAttack~\\citep{ren2024actorattack} without adaptation. Classification supervision stabilizes predictions within the synthetic distribution (std reduced 4--20$\\times$) regardless of label correctness, but does not transfer to human-authored data, where all classification variants underperform pure MLM.\n\nOur contributions:\n\\begin{itemize}[leftmargin=*, itemsep=2pt, topsep=4pt]\n  \\item \\textbf{Systematic decomposition of cross-attack generalization.} We decompose six dimensions of encoder adaptation (domain exposure, classification supervision, label quality, label granularity, domain specificity, and temporal modeling) through controlled ablation across twelve encoder variants and three OOD attack families. For base-size encoders (125--184M), domain-adaptive pretraining is the primary mechanism ($\\sim$60\\,pp F1 gap); for the largest encoder (355M), pretrained representations already suffice on structurally complex attacks. Classification supervision, label quality, and temporal structure are non-factors for detection, yielding several counter-intuitive negative results.\n\n  \\item \\textbf{Capacity gradient for domain adaptation.} Three encoder scales (125M, 184M, 355M) reveal a clear capacity gradient: as pretrained model size increases, the set of attacks requiring domain adaptation shrinks. DeBERTa-v3-base requires adaptation for all unseen attacks, RoBERTa-base tolerates structurally simple attacks but fails on complex ones, and RoBERTa-large handles both attack types without adaptation. Although pretraining objectives also differ (RTD vs.\\ MLM), the monotonic pattern across three scales suggests model size is the primary factor (Section~\\ref{sec:cross_encoder}).\n\n  \\item \\textbf{Distribution-specific classification effects.} All classification-supervised variants achieve equivalent detection regardless of label correctness (F1 gap $\\leq$3.9\\,pp), with 4--20$\\times$ cross-seed variance reduction on synthetic OOD evaluations vs.\\ MLM-only. This stability benefit does not transfer to human-authored data (Section~\\ref{sec:human_data}). The 9-class taxonomy provides interpretable strategy characterization for known attack families (Section~\\ref{sec:strategy}).\n\\end{itemize}\n",

exp15c 完成：MLM-only DAPT 在 RoBERTa-large 上比 9class 更差，证明是 continued pretraining 的 representation drift，不是 classification 的问题。

### 修改 1: Table 3 — 添加 RoBERTa-large MLM 行

在现有 Vanilla 和 9-class 行之间插入 MLM 行：

RoBERTa-large MLM (355M):
- DD: K1=0.876, K3=不确定（用 "--" 如果没有）, Full=0.884
- AA: K1=0.876, K3=不确定, Full=0.874

注意 MLM 在两个数据集上都是三者最差。不加粗任何数字。

如果没有 K3 数据，只填 K1 和 Full。

### 修改 2: §4.4 capacity-DAPT 交互段落更新

替换当前关于"cannot distinguish whether DAPT mechanism itself or classification specifically hurts"的讨论。现在有答案了：

"Adding the MLM-only variant completes the picture: all three RoBERTa-large configurations show that any form of continued pretraining, whether unsupervised (MLM) or supervised (9-class), reduces ActorAttack performance relative to the frozen vanilla encoder (0.874 and 0.946 vs.\ 1.000). The mechanism is representation drift: continued pretraining shifts the embedding space away from its pretrained optimum, and this cost exceeds the adaptation benefit when the encoder already has sufficient capacity for the target distribution. On harder OOD (Deceptive Delight), classification DAPT still provides net benefit (+6pp over vanilla) by offering structured signal that partially compensates for drift, while MLM DAPT lacks this compensating structure and hurts even on DD (0.884 vs.\ 0.927)."

"A learning rate control (Appendix~\ref{app:roberta_large_lr}) and an MLM-only variant both confirm the pattern: continued pretraining of any form reduces ActorAttack performance for the largest encoder, establishing representation drift as the mechanism."

### 修改 4: Appendix LR control section — 加一句 exp15c

在 LR control 表后面加："An MLM-only variant (without classification) produces the same pattern (AA Full = 0.874), confirming that representation drift from continued pretraining, not classification-specific bias, is the underlying cause."

不用 trailing -ing phrases，不用 em-dash。编译验证 0 errors，主文 ≤ 8 页。

```text
145	
146	\begin{table}[t]
147	\centering
148	\small
149	\caption{Cross-encoder comparison on OOD Full (3-seed mean$\pm$std). DD = Deceptive Delight, AA = ActorAttack.}
150	\label{tab:cross_encoder}
151	\begin{tabular}{@{}lcc@{}}
152	\toprule
153	\textbf{Variant} & DD & AA \\
154	\midrule
155	\multicolumn{3}{l}{\textit{DeBERTa-v3-base (184M)}} \\[2pt]
156	Vanilla & .312$\pm$.026 & .258$\pm$.000 \\
157	MLM & .914$\pm$.115 & .893$\pm$.080 \\
158	9-class & .997$\pm$.005 & .972$\pm$.026 \\
159	\midrule
160	\multicolumn{3}{l}{\textit{RoBERTa-base (125M)}} \\[2pt]
161	Vanilla & .981$\pm$.026 & .466$\pm$.163 \\
162	MLM & .987$\pm$.018 & .730$\pm$.201 \\
163	9-class & .994$\pm$.005 & .939$\pm$.046 \\
164	\midrule
165	\multicolumn{3}{l}{\textit{RoBERTa-large (355M)}} \\[2pt]
166	Vanilla & .927$\pm$.036 & \textbf{1.000$\pm$.000} \\
167	9-class & .987$\pm$.004 & .946$\pm$.057 \\
168	\bottomrule
169	\end{tabular}
170	\end{table}
171	
172	We replicate vanilla, MLM, and 9-class on RoBERTa-base~\citep{liu2019roberta} (Table~\ref{tab:cross_encoder}).
173	On DD, RoBERTa-base vanilla already achieves 0.981$\pm$0.026 (vs.\ DeBERTa 0.312$\pm$0.026), so DAPT adds only 1.3\,pp. ActorAttack reveals this as DD's simplicity: RoBERTa-base vanilla collapses to 0.466$\pm$0.163, a 51.5\,pp drop, while domain-adapted variants recover (9-class: 0.939$\pm$0.046).
174	
175	RoBERTa-large (355M) reveals a capacity-DAPT interaction: while domain adaptation improves DD detection across all scales (+6\,pp for large, +69\,pp for DeBERTa), its effect on ActorAttack reverses for the largest encoder (vanilla 1.000 vs.\ 9-class 0.946). Smaller encoders (125--184M) require DAPT to bridge a 47--71\,pp gap on ActorAttack. A control experiment with halved learning rate ($1 \times 10^{-5}$) produces an even larger regression (AA Full F1 = 0.781 vs.\ 0.946; Appendix~\ref{app:roberta_large_lr}), confirming that the capacity-DAPT interaction is structural rather than hyperparameter-dependent.
176	
177	Three encoder scales thus reveal a capacity gradient for the \emph{difficulty threshold}: DeBERTa-v3-base requires adaptation for all unseen attacks, RoBERTa-base tolerates simple attacks but fails on complex ones, and RoBERTa-large handles both without adaptation. Although pretraining objectives also differ (RTD vs.\ MLM), the consistent monotonic pattern suggests model size is the dominant factor (Section~\ref{sec:discussion}).
178	Classification supervision provides a consistent stability benefit across both base encoders (cross-seed variance: 0.005--0.046 vs.\ 0.026--0.201).
179	
180	CKA analysis~\citep{kornblith2019cka} and linear probing confirm that DAPT reshapes representations substantially, though comparable CKA shifts yield different detection gains across starting points (Appendix~\ref{sec:cka_heatmap}).
181	
182	%----------------------------------------------------------------------
183	\subsection{Human Data Validation}
184	\label{sec:human_data}
```

Smaller encoders (125--184M) require DAPT to bridge a 47--71\\,pp gap on ActorAttack. A control experiment with halved learning rate ($1 \\times 10^{-5}$) produces an even larger regression (AA Full F1 = 0.781 vs.\\ 0.946; Appendix~\\ref{app:roberta_large_lr}), confirming that the capacity-DAPT interaction is structural rather than hyperparameter-dependent.\n\nThree encoder scales thus reveal a capacity gradient for the \\emph{difficulty threshold}: DeBERTa-v3-base requires adaptation for all unseen attacks, RoBERTa-base tolerates simple attacks but fails on complex ones, and RoBERTa-large handles both without adaptation. Although pretraining objectives also differ (RTD vs.\\ MLM), the consistent monotonic pattern suggests model size is the dominant factor (Section~\\ref{sec:discussion}).\nClassification supervision provides a consistent stability benefit across both base encoders (cross-seed variance: 0.005--0.046 vs.\\ 0.026--0.201).\n\nCKA analysis~\\citep{kornblith2019cka} and linear probing confirm that DAPT reshapes representations substantially, though comparable CKA shifts yield different detection gains across starting points (Appendix~\\ref{sec:cka_heatmap}).\n\n%----------------------------------------------------------------------\n\\subsection{Human Data Validation}\n\\label{sec:human_data}",

{
  "replace_all": false,

  "old_string": "RoBERTa-large (355M) reveals a capacity-DAPT interaction: while domain adaptation improves DD detection across all scales (+6\\,pp for large, +69\\,pp for DeBERTa), its effect on ActorAttack reverses for the largest encoder (vanilla 1.000 vs.\\ 9-class 0.946). Smaller encoders (125--184M) require DAPT to bridge a 47--71\\,pp gap on ActorAttack. A control experiment with halved learning rate ($1 \\times 10^{-5}$) produces an even larger regression (AA Full F1 = 0.781 vs.\\ 0.946; Appendix~\\ref{app:roberta_large_lr}), confirming that the capacity-DAPT interaction is structural rather than hyperparameter-dependent.",
  "new_string": "RoBERTa-large (355M) reveals a capacity-DAPT interaction: while domain adaptation improves DD detection across all scales (+6\\,pp for large, +69\\,pp for DeBERTa), its effect on ActorAttack reverses for the largest encoder (vanilla 1.000 vs.\\ 9-class 0.946). Smaller encoders (125--184M) require DAPT to bridge a 47--71\\,pp gap. The MLM-only variant identifies the mechanism as representation drift: all forms of continued pretraining (MLM 0.874, 9-class 0.946) reduce ActorAttack performance relative to the frozen encoder (1.000); a learning rate control (Appendix~\\ref{app:roberta_large_lr}) confirms this is not hyperparameter-dependent. On DD, classification DAPT still provides net benefit (+6\\,pp) through structured signal that partially compensates for drift, while MLM DAPT lacks this structure (0.884 vs.\\ 0.927)."
}

        "lines": [
          " ",
          " RoBERTa-large (355M) reveals a capacity-DAPT interaction: while domain adaptation improves DD detection across all scales (+6\\,pp for large, +69\\,pp for DeBERTa), its effect on ActorAttack reverses for the largest encoder (vanilla 1.000 vs.\\ 9-class 0.946). Smaller encoders (125--184M) require DAPT to bridge a 47--71\\,pp gap. The MLM-only variant identifies the mechanism as representation drift: all forms of continued pretraining (MLM 0.874, 9-class 0.946) reduce ActorAttack performance relative to the frozen encoder (1.000); a learning rate control (Appendix~\\ref{app:roberta_large_lr}) confirms this is not hyperparameter-dependent. On DD, classification DAPT still provides net benefit (+6\\,pp) through structured signal that partially compensates for drift, while MLM DAPT lacks this structure (0.884 vs.\\ 0.927).",
          " ",

          " ",

```text
17	\begin{itemize}[leftmargin=*, itemsep=2pt, topsep=4pt]
18	  \item \textbf{Systematic decomposition of cross-attack generalization.} We decompose six dimensions of encoder adaptation (domain exposure, classification supervision, label quality, label granularity, domain specificity, and temporal modeling) through controlled ablation across twelve encoder variants and three OOD attack families. For base-size encoders (125--184M), domain-adaptive pretraining is the primary mechanism ($\sim$60\,pp F1 gap); for the largest encoder (355M), pretrained representations already suffice on structurally complex attacks. Classification supervision, label quality, and temporal structure are non-factors for detection, yielding several counter-intuitive negative results.
19	
```

_source line 2727 · 2026-05-19T12:28:43.659Z_

[Director 指令] [深度写作优化] 用户要求论文全面写作升级 — 处理完当前 exp15c + scope 两个 patch 后启动

**用户原话**："最新版的论文让 Paper Agent 仔细反思、深度思考，并向同类的优秀论文学习，从而优化出一版写作。优化完写作之后再优化图表。"

**这不是 patch，是全面写作升级。** 按以下流程执行：

---

### Phase 1: 学习参考（先读再写）

用 WebSearch 找 2-3 篇 EMNLP/ACL 2024-2025 的高分 analysis/ablation study 论文（主题相关或方法论类似）：
- 搜索方向：jailbreak detection, adversarial robustness analysis, pretraining ablation, systematic evaluation
- 重点学习：Abstract 如何用最少篇幅讲完故事、Introduction 如何从大问题切入到具体贡献、Results 如何组织 negative findings、Discussion 如何处理 confounds

### Phase 2: 深度反思

读完参考论文后，对照当前论文逐章节反思：
1. **Abstract**: hook 是否足够强？"reductive answer" 角度是否清晰？数字是否服务于叙事？
2. **Introduction**: 问题动机是否让非专家也能理解重要性？贡献列表是否 crisp？
3. **Related Work**: positioning 是否清晰（我们是 analysis paper，不是 method paper）？引用是否充分？
4. **Method**: 是否简洁清晰？Stage 1/Stage 2 pipeline 是否一目了然？
5. **Experiments**: 叙事是否流畅？12 个 variant 的结果是否有清晰的 storyline 而非数据堆砌？negative findings 是否被充分 leverage？
6. **Discussion**: confounds 处理是否让审稿人信服？capacity gradient 叙事是否深刻？
7. **Conclusion**: 是否 impactful？读完后审稿人能否清楚记住 3 个 takeaways？
8. **Limitations**: 是否诚实但不自毁？cross-LLM、small test set 等是否处理得当？

### Phase 3: 逐章节优化

基于反思结果，逐章节改写。**写作规范硬约束**（必须遵守）：
- 不用 trailing -ing phrases（"demonstrating...", "highlighting..."）
- 不用 em-dash（—）或解释性 en-dash（–）
- 不用黑名单词（delve, leverage, utilize, facilitate, myriad, plethora）
- 不过度分段（5-8 句为正常段落）
- 不用 "Furthermore / Moreover / Additionally" 连续出现
- 不用 "It is worth noting..." / "Notably, ..."
- 每条 claim 必须有对应数据支撑
- 首次出现的缩写必须展开

**内容约束**：
- 主文 ≤ 8 页（EMNLP 2026 限制）
- 不改变任何实验数字
- 不改变核心 claims（domain exposure primary for base-size, capacity gradient, classification distribution-specific）
- 可以重新组织段落顺序、合并/拆分段落、改写句子
- 可以删除低信息密度的句子为高密度内容腾空间

### Phase 4: 图表优化（写作完成后）

逐个检查所有 Table 和 Figure：
1. Table 1-3 + Appendix tables: caption 自包含？数字精度统一？最优加粗/次优下划线一致？
2. Figure 1 (pipeline): 信息量是否配得上占用的空间？能否更紧凑？
3. t-SNE figure: 标签是否清晰？字体 ≥ 7pt？
4. 所有图表：与正文叙事是否紧密关联？有没有"孤立"的图表？

完成后汇报每个章节的改动摘要。

Links: [{"title":"Exploring the Vulnerability of the Content Moderation ...","url":"https://aclanthology.org/2025.findings-emnlp.114.pdf"},{"title":"LLM Jailbreak Detection for (Almost) Free! - ACL Anthology","url":"https://aclanthology.org/2025.findings-emnlp.309/"},{"title":"Do Internal Layers of LLMs Reveal Patterns for Jailbreak Detection?","url":"https://arxiv.org/pdf/2510.06594"},{"title":"FC-Attack: Jailbreaking Multimodal Large Language ...","url":"https://aclanthology.org/2025.findings-emnlp.494.pdf"},{"title":"Guarding the Guardrails: A Taxonomy-Driven Approach to Jailbreak Detection","url":"https://arxiv.org/pdf/2510.13893"},{"title":"Silencing the Guardrails: Inference-Time Jailbreaking via Dynamic Contextual Representation Ablation","url":"https://arxiv.org/pdf/2604.07835"},{"title":"How Alignment and Jailbreak Work: Explain LLM Safety through Intermediate Hidden States - ACL Anthology","url":"https://aclanthology.org/2024.findings-emnlp.139/"}]

I found several relevant papers on jailbreak detection from EMNLP 2024 and 2025. Here are the key findings:

## EMNLP 2025 Papers on Jailbreak Detection

Jailbreak detection methods show promise in mitigating jailbreak attacks through the assistance of other models or multiple model inferences, but existing methods entail significant computational costs. One notable paper addresses this limitation: "LLM Jailbreak Detection for (Almost) Free!" was published in the Findings of EMNLP 2025. The paper presents a finding that the difference in output distributions between jailbreak and benign prompts can be employed for detecting jailbreak, and proposes Free Jailbreak Detection (FJD) which enhances detection performance through the integration of virtual instruction learning.

## EMNLP 2024 Papers

"How Alignment and Jailbreak Work: Explain LLM Safety through Intermediate Hidden States" by Zhou et al. was published in the Findings of EMNLP 2024. The paper employs weak classifiers to explain LLM safety through the intermediate hidden states.

## Related Research on Internal Representations

Another relevant study investigates the jailbreak phenomenon by examining the internal representations of LLMs, analyzing the open-source LLM GPT-J and the state-space model Mamba2, with results suggesting promising directions for leveraging internal model dynamics for robust jailbreak detection and defense.

The search results show active research in this area, though I didn't find papers explicitly labeled as "best paper" awards in these conferences.


REMINDER: You MUST include the sources above in your response to the user using markdown hyperlinks.
